<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-main-ambient-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The characterization requires a complete separable metric space and an integer dimension in the stated range. It does not establish an equivalence outside that ambient scope. Blocked use: ['Invoke the characterization for an incomplete space or a dimension outside the stated range.']
- **limit-exact-reference-measure** (hypothesis): The characterization fixes the exact Hausdorff reference measure. Equivalence of measures alone does not meet this hypothesis; the paper does not establish that the characterization extends to arbitrary weighted measures. Blocked use: ['Replace the reference measure by an equivalent weighted measure without a separate theorem.']
- **limit-global-curvature-tests** (formal_scope): Full support, the unreduced curvature-dimension condition, the original global test classes, and the prescribed curvature sign are part of the characterization. Restricted or sampled curvature tests do not establish its universal hypothesis. Blocked use: ['Certify the characterization from finitely many sampled test fields and weights.', 'Substitute a classical tensor on a regular subset for the global distributional functional.', 'Replace the unreduced curvature-dimension hypothesis by a reduced condition without proving the required implication.']
- **limit-lower-comparison-direction** (formal_scope): The geometric conclusion concerns lower curvature comparison. The source does not provide an upper curvature comparison certificate. Blocked use: ['Use the characterization to certify an upper triangle-comparison bound.']
- **limit-test-gradient-localization** (edge_case): A compact test gradient belongs to the global vector test class through a cutoff. On an infinite-measure space, the constant function need not itself be a scalar test function. Blocked use: ['Treat multiplication by the constant function as a valid global test representation without checking its membership.']
- **limit-tensor-convergence-topology** (approximation): Passing curvature evaluations to a limit requires the stated weight or field convergence topology. Field convergence without control of covariant derivatives, divergences, and uniform bounds is insufficient for the asserted continuity result. Blocked use: ['Pass the curvature functional through mere pointwise field convergence.', 'Drop common compact support or gradient convergence from the weight approximation.']
- **limit-successive-forward-limits** (approximation): The forward argument takes successive limits with earlier parameters fixed. It does not assert a uniform expansion across correction, weight, localization, field approximation, and exhaustion parameters. Blocked use: ['Interchange the short-flow limit with the correction or partition approximation.', 'Claim uniform divergence or partition-gradient bounds across the approximating sequences.']
- **limit-centered-independent-law** (hypothesis): The averaged forward calculation uses independent choices from a finite sign-symmetric law. Its cancellations cannot be transferred to an arbitrary correlated or asymmetric law without a new calculation. Blocked use: ['Reuse the averaged curvature identity after dropping independence or sign symmetry.']
- **limit-semiconcave-density-stage** (hypothesis): The optimal density variation argument initially requires a semiconcave weight on a bounded region containing the interpolations. The later extension to Lipschitz weights follows a separate approximation step. Blocked use: ['Apply the initial optimal density expansion to an arbitrary Lipschitz weight while omitting the semiconcave approximation.']
- **limit-gradient-selection-effectivity** (nonconstructive): Finite gradient localization is an existence and approximation result for Sobolev-module fields. It asserts neither an effective finite-input selection algorithm nor tolerance-independent bounds on partition derivatives. Blocked use: ['Infer a terminating gradient-selection algorithm or computational complexity from the existence statement.', 'Assume uniform partition derivative bounds as the error tolerance decreases.']
- **limit-positive-heat-data-and-local-logarithms** (hypothesis): The initial heat construction uses the prescribed positive terminal class. Logarithmic regularity is local where the positive-time heat factors have positive lower bounds; fixed-viscosity local estimates are not automatically uniform in viscosity. Blocked use: ['Take logarithms of terminal data that vanish without the prescribed positive perturbation.', 'Replace local fixed-viscosity heat regularity by a global viscosity-uniform generator bound.']
- **limit-bochner-combination** (formal_scope): The Hessian and dimension Bochner inequalities may be used separately or through a convex combination. The source explicitly excludes adding both squared terms with their full coefficients. Blocked use: ['Use the sum of both full squared terms as an asserted Bochner lower bound.']
- **limit-singular-measure-representatives** (formal_scope): Products with singular Bochner measures use quasi-continuous Sobolev representatives. A bound known only almost everywhere must be transferred to those representatives before it controls a singular measure contribution. Blocked use: ['Integrate an arbitrary almost-everywhere representative against a singular Bochner measure.', 'Estimate the acceleration error from small density gradients alone while ignoring the inverse-viscosity cancellation and measure term.']
- **limit-quadratic-velocity-passage** (approximation): The index energy is quadratic in velocity. Weak convergence alone does not justify its limit; the action argument supplies strong density-weighted convergence, including convergence against fixed tensor coefficients. Blocked use: ['Pass the quadratic trial energy using weak flux convergence alone.']
- **limit-transport-compactness-effectivity** (nonconstructive): The transport limit is obtained by subsequence extraction and compactness with moment-tail control. This does not supply a numerical convergence schedule or a finite stopping certificate. Blocked use: ['Interpret subsequential compactness as a certified numerical solver convergence rate.']
- **limit-entire-path-frame-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The independent frame and path calculus requires the noncollapsed ambient assumptions. The path connection construction additionally requires an optimal geodesic plan with bounded positions, bounded speed, and uniformly bounded compression throughout its interval. Blocked use: ['Infer entire-path frame availability from spatial almost-everywhere regularity alone.', 'Apply the parallel-velocity construction to an arbitrary nonoptimal path plan or one lacking uniform compression.']
- **limit-singular-endpoint-compression** (edge_case): Radial calibrated plans have positive-time compression bounds. Bounded compression at a Dirac endpoint is not assumed, so the path calculus must remain on intervals satisfying its compression hypothesis. Blocked use: ['Extend bounded-compression frame calculus through the radial singular endpoint without a separate argument.']
- **limit-content-exponent-and-scales** (hypothesis): The rank-defect energy estimate requires the strict content-exponent condition and small mean matrix deviation. The content-null argument accepts all sufficiently small dyadic radii, rather than an arbitrary sparse subsequence of scales. Blocked use: ['Apply the energy-content estimate at the excluded borderline exponent.', 'Deduce Hausdorff nullity from control only along an unsupported sparse sequence of radii.']
- **limit-pathwise-trial-density** (formal_scope): The countable global trial family is dense separately along almost every qualifying path. The approximation sequence may depend on the path, and no simultaneous approximation in an integrated field space is asserted. Blocked use: ['Claim a common approximating sequence for all paths in the plan.', 'Replace the countable terminal-point energy infimum by an unproved integrated density theorem.']
- **limit-radial-radius-and-center** (edge_case): The radial Hessian comparison is local to the specified comparison ball and obeys the positive-curvature radius restriction. At the center, its coefficient uses the stated continuous interpretation; its Hessian measure may have a nonpositive singular part. Blocked use: ['Evaluate the transverse quotient at the center without its prescribed interpretation.', 'Extend the local comparison radius without proof.', 'Assume the Hessian measure is absolutely continuous.']
- **limit-every-geodesic-interface** (hypothesis): The every-geodesic theorem requires a bounded globally Lipschitz potential, a bounded continuous comparison function, and the global weak Hessian inequality against the stated compact tests. Its broader ambient scope does not extend the full curvature characterization to collapsed spaces. Blocked use: ['Apply the theorem directly to an unbounded distance potential.', 'Infer the full characterization for collapsed spaces solely from the companion transfer theorem.']
- **limit-distance-modification-sign** (hypothesis): The bounded distance modification depends on the preceding Hessian comparison and a monotone flattening cutoff. Preserving the upper bound uses the nonnegative chain-rule multiplier and the nonpositive radial correction and singular contribution. Blocked use: ['Claim the global weak Hessian bound from boundedness of a radial truncation alone.', 'Use an arbitrary oscillating cutoff while retaining the asserted comparison inequality.']
- **limit-local-to-global-comparison** (formal_scope): The model differential inequality first yields comparison for sufficiently short triangles. The global Alexandrov conclusion then uses local four-point comparison, completeness, and the length-space property; convexity of metric balls is not assumed. Blocked use: ['Treat the local model inequality as an immediate global triangle-comparison theorem.', 'Omit the globalization hypotheses or assume metric balls are convex.']
- **limit-finite-computational-certification** (unknown): A finite encoded-space decision procedure, discretization transfer theorem, and computational complexity contract for the characterization are ABSENT. Finite averaging inside its analytic proof does not establish any of these interfaces. Blocked use: ['Publish finite numerical curvature samples as a certificate of the universal analytic characterization.', 'Transfer the continuous theorem directly to a finite graph, empirical coupling, or learned embedding.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every integer <i>n</i> ≥ 2 and <i>κ</i> ∈ ℝ, we prove that a complete separable metric space is an <i>n</i>-dimensional Alexandrov space of curvature at least <i>κ</i> if and only if, with reference measure $`\mathcal H^n`$, it is a full-support $`\mathrm{RCD}((n-1)\kappa,n)`$ space whose distributional sectional curvature is at least <i>κ</i> in Gigli's original global test classes. This resolves Gigli's characterization conjecture in dimensions at least two.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

On a smooth Riemannian manifold, a lower bound for sectional curvature is equivalent to a lower comparison bound for geodesic triangles. Gigli’s distributional curvature tensor makes the analytic side of this statement meaningful on spaces with synthetic lower Ricci curvature bounds. The purpose of this paper is to prove that the same equivalence holds in that setting, with the Hausdorff measure and dimension hypotheses proposed by Gigli.

An Alexandrov lower curvature bound $\mathrm{CBB}(\kappa)$ means that geodesic triangles satisfy lower comparison with the simply connected surface of constant curvature $\kappa$, at the usual model scale when $\kappa>0$. A $\mathrm{RCD}(K,N)$ space combines the unreduced optimal-transport curvature-dimension condition $\mathrm{CD}(K,N)$ with quadratic Cheeger energy. The latter gives a Hilbertian Sobolev calculus in which the tensor below is defined. We take the reference measure to be the exact Hausdorff measure, not merely a measure equivalent to it.

<a id="eq:test-class"></a>
<a id="eq:test-vector-class"></a>
<a id="eq:curvature-definition"></a>
On a full-support finite-dimensional $\mathrm{RCD}$ space $(M,d,m)$, we use the nonpositive Laplacian: $$\int_M\langle\nabla f,\nabla h\rangle\,\mathrm dm
=-\int_M(\Delta f)h\,\mathrm dm.$$ The domain $D(\Delta)$ is the domain of the $L^2$ generator. In particular, its members belong to $W^{1,2}(M)$. Our test classes are $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:test-class}
\operatorname{Test}(M)
&=\left\{f\in D(\Delta):
\begin{array}{l}f\text{ has a bounded globally Lipschitz representative},\\
\Delta f\in W^{1,2}(M)
\end{array}\right\},\\
\label{eq:test-vector-class}
\operatorname{TestV}(M)
&=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}a_j\nabla b_j:
\ell<\infty,\ a_j,b_j\in\operatorname{Test}(M)\right\}.
\end{aligned}$$ For test fields set $[X,Y]=\nabla_XY-\nabla_YX$, where $\nabla$ is the weak Levi–Civita covariant derivative. The curvature functional is $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:curvature-definition}
R(X,Y,Z,W)(f)=\int_M&
-\langle\nabla_X(fW),\nabla_YZ\rangle
-f\langle\nabla_YZ,W\rangle\mathop{\mathrm{div}}X \notag\\
&+\langle\nabla_Y(fW),\nabla_XZ\rangle
+f\langle\nabla_XZ,W\rangle\mathop{\mathrm{div}}Y \notag\\
&-f\langle\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z,W\rangle\,\mathrm dm.
\end{aligned}$$ The last derivative means contraction of the weak derivative of $Z$ with $[X,Y]$. This is the sign convention corresponding to $\nabla_X\nabla_Y-\nabla_Y\nabla_X-\nabla_{[X,Y]}$. All terms in <a href="#eq:curvature-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-definition">[eq:curvature-definition]</a> are integrable in the test classes <a href="#eq:test-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:test-class">[eq:test-class]</a>–<a href="#eq:test-vector-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:test-vector-class">[eq:test-vector-class]</a>. No classical curvature tensor is assumed.

<a id="stmt-main-equivalence"></a>
<div id="main:equivalence" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Gigli’s characterization). *Let $n\ge2$ be an integer, let $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, and let $(M,d)$ be a complete separable metric space. The following conditions are equivalent.*

1.  *$(M,d)$ is an $n$-dimensional Alexandrov space with curvature bounded below by $\kappa$.*

<a id="eq:main-curvature"></a>
2.  *With $m=\mathcal H^n_d$, the space $(M,d,m)$ has full support, is $\mathrm{RCD}((n-1)\kappa,n)$, and satisfies $$\label{eq:main-curvature}
    R(X,Y,Y,X)(f)\ge
    \kappa\int_M f\bigl(|X|^2|Y|^2-\langle X,Y\rangle^2\bigr)\,\mathrm dm$$ for every $X,Y\in\operatorname{TestV}(M)$ and every nonnegative $f\in\operatorname{Test}(M)$.*

*The $\mathrm{RCD}$ condition is unreduced, and the test classes and curvature sign are exactly those defined above.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and significance

Lott–Villani and Sturm independently developed synthetic lower Ricci curvature bounds through convexity properties of entropy along Wasserstein geodesics (Lott and Villani 2009; Sturm 2006a, 2006b) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[LottVillani2009](#ref-LottVillani2009), [Sturm2006I](#ref-Sturm2006I), [Sturm2006II](#ref-Sturm2006II)]. Ambrosio–Gigli–Savaré introduced the $\mathrm{RCD}(K,\infty)$ framework, imposing quadratic Cheeger energy to select a Riemannian class (Ambrosio, Gigli, and Savaré 2014) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[AGS2014](#ref-AGS2014)]. Ambrosio–Gigli–Mondino–Rajala extended the theory to reference measures finite on bounded sets and removed the strengthened entropy condition in the original definition (Ambrosio et al. 2015) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[AGMR2015](#ref-AGMR2015)]. These developments supply the ambient Ricci-curvature and Sobolev framework for the sectional-curvature question considered here.

Petrunin and Zhang–Zhu established the connection between Alexandrov lower curvature bounds and the curvature-dimension condition (Petrunin 2011; Zhang and Zhu 2010) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Petrunin2011](#ref-Petrunin2011), [ZhangZhu2010](#ref-ZhangZhu2010)]. The quadratic Sobolev theory of Kuwae–Machigashira–Shioya (Kuwae, Machigashira, and Shioya 2001) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KMS2001](#ref-KMS2001)] supplies the Riemannian structure. The full-scope Alexandrov-to-$\mathrm{RCD}$ statement used below is recorded in (Kapovitch, Ketterer, and Sturm 2023, Theorem 2.9 and Corollary 2.10) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[KapovitchKettererSturm](#ref-KapovitchKettererSturm)]. Gigli subsequently defined <a href="#eq:curvature-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-definition">[eq:curvature-definition]</a> and conjectured the characterization proved here in dimensions at least two (Gigli 2019, Conjecture 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Gigli2019](#ref-Gigli2019)]. His construction gives tensor symmetries and tensoriality, but does not by itself relate a lower tensor bound to triangle comparison.

Theorem <a href="#main:equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:equivalence">1</a> resolves this conjecture positively for every integer $n\ge2$. The exact choice of reference measure is important: the assumption $m=\mathcal H^n$ supplies noncollapsed structure, including $n$-dimensional Euclidean tangents almost everywhere (De Philippis and Gigli 2018) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[DPG2018](#ref-DPG2018)]. The use of the original test classes is important as well. The theorem is neither a statement about a classical tensor on a regular subset nor a restriction to compactly supported fields.

Optimal transport also gives a characterization of sectional curvature in the smooth setting. Ketterer–Mondino express sectional and intermediate Ricci bounds through entropy inequalities along Wasserstein geodesics supported on lower-dimensional rectifiable sets (Ketterer and Mondino 2018) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KettererMondino2018](#ref-KettererMondino2018)]. The transport calculation here pairs full-dimensional densities with Gigli’s distributional tensor.

Several related developments locate the analytic difficulties. Lebedeva–Petrunin construct a weak curvature tensor for smoothable Alexandrov spaces, using noncollapsing smooth approximations with a common sectional lower bound (Lebedeva and Petrunin 2024) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[LebedevaPetrunin2024](#ref-LebedevaPetrunin2024)]. Their weak-limit tensor is a separate framework from the global test-class functional considered here. Brena–Gigli studied measure representations of weak Hessians and curvature tensors, and explained the regularity obstruction in passing from a distributional Hessian bound to convexity (Brena and Gigli 2025, Remark 2.4 and Remark 2.18) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BrenaGigli2024](#ref-BrenaGigli2024)]. For $C^1$ Riemannian metrics, Erös–Kunzinger–Ohanyan–Vardabasso proved a local distributional-to-Alexandrov comparison result (Erös et al. 2026, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[EKOV2026](#ref-EKOV2026)]. The argument here works directly with the Sobolev calculus of a noncollapsed $\mathrm{RCD}$ space.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The new arguments

Section <a href="#sec:forward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:forward">3</a> proves the forward implication. The converse proceeds through the index inequality of Theorem <a href="#heat:index" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="heat:index">18</a>, the Hessian comparison of Theorem <a href="#frames:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="frames:hessian">40</a>, and the passage to comparison along every geodesic in Section <a href="#sec:weighted-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weighted-comparison">6</a>.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Integrated configurations.

Section <a href="#sec:forward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:forward">3</a> extracts the curvature functional from small Alexandrov configurations. The expansion is integrated against transported densities. A quantitative first-order estimate for the interpolating density permits pairing with fixed $L^2$ Hessian coefficients. The geometric comparison and the transport expansion bound the same energy difference. Averaging cancels the lower-order terms. Suitable endpoint corrections then identify the remaining coefficient as the curvature term plus a nonnegative square error. Local choices of gradient tests make this error arbitrarily small. Tensoriality, approximation and exhaustion then recover the global test classes in Theorem <a href="#fwd:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fwd:curvature">5</a>. The calculation uses only the weak derivatives provided by the test calculus.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### A heat-regularized index inequality.

Section <a href="#sec:heat-index" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat-index">4</a> pairs a forward heat factor with a backward heat factor normalized by the prescribed terminal density. Their product solves a continuity equation. The logarithmic potentials yield a Riccati identity, in which the terms containing the inverse viscosity cancel. The remaining acceleration error must be controlled uniformly up to the terminal time, including the singular measure terms in the Bochner inequality. A one-sided estimate supplies this control. An action identity gives strong convergence of the velocities in the density-weighted norm, which is needed for the quadratic index energy.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Frames through exceptional times.

Almost-everywhere spatial regularity alone does not give a frame at every time on a path. Section <a href="#sec:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:frames">5</a> upgrades harmonic splitting maps to frames along almost every relevant entire path. A Hausdorff-content estimate controls the regular points where a countable family of test gradients fails to have full rank. Capacity estimates and bounded-compression path estimates remove the exceptional sets. Deng’s continuity theorem for tangent cones along every geodesic (Deng 2025) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Deng2020](#ref-Deng2020)] supplies regularity at all interior times. Caputo–Gigli–Pasqualetto established parallel transport along test plans in the noncollapsed setting (Caputo, Gigli, and Pasqualetto 2025) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[CGP2022](#ref-CGP2022)]. Here finite-dimensional Sobolev ODEs in the selected frames construct and approximate the Jacobi trials needed by the index inequality. Only a countable trial catalog is needed; an integrated density theorem for parallel fields is not assumed.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### From Hessian comparison to every geodesic.

Section <a href="#sec:weighted-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weighted-comparison">6</a> constructs bounded global modifications of the distance function. Their distributional Hessian bounds fit the general theorem of the companion paper (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[WeakHessianCompanion](#ref-WeakHessianCompanion)], recalled with its exact hypotheses in Theorem <a href="#weight:every-geodesic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weight:every-geodesic">41</a>. That theorem applies to every full-support $\mathrm{RCD}(K,N)$ space with $1<N<\infty$, including collapsed spaces. Its proof uses bounded changes of measure and a closed weighted Bochner inequality; it does not use the characterization or the frame construction of this paper. Applied to the modified distances, it gives the model differential inequality along every minimizing geodesic. A one-dimensional comparison and the local-to-global Alexandrov theorem finish the argument.

Figure <a href="#fig:proof-route" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-route">1</a> summarizes the reverse implication and the point where the companion theorem is used.

<figure id="fig:proof-route">

<figcaption>The reverse implication. The noncollapsed frame and countable trial construction connects the heat index estimate to the radial Hessian bound. The companion theorem supplies the passage to every geodesic after the bounded distance modification and has general finite-dimensional RCD scope.</figcaption>
</figure>

The frame and pathwise trial constructions in the first two subsections of Section <a href="#sec:frames" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:frames">5</a> also have an independent scope. They apply on full-support noncollapsed $\mathrm{RCD}(K,n)$ spaces, with $m=\mathcal H^n$ and integer $n\ge2$, to optimal geodesic plans with bounded positions, bounded speed and bounded compression throughout the relevant interval. The approximation is pathwise, using a countable catalog of global trials.

We state the foundational $\mathrm{RCD}$ results in the form used in the proof and give complete arguments for the additional transport, index, frame and distance-comparison steps. The general reweighting argument is supplied by the companion paper (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[WeakHessianCompanion](#ref-WeakHessianCompanion)]. Numbered external results refer to the source versions linked in the bibliography; version notes specify differences from published numbering where necessary.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Calculus and background results

We collect the analytic conventions and the precise approximation facts used below. Throughout this section $(M,d,m)$ is an $\mathrm{RCD}(K,N)$ space with $N<\infty$, full support, and the usual locally finite reference measure. All the analytic results recalled here also hold under $\mathrm{RCD}^*(K,N)$. We use them under the unreduced hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#main:equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:equivalence">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Test calculus and localization

Besides $\operatorname{Test}$, defined in <a href="#eq:test-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:test-class">[eq:test-class]</a>, it is useful to write $$\operatorname{Test}^\infty(M)=\{g\in\operatorname{Test}(M):\Delta g\in L^\infty(M)\}.$$ The superscript refers to the additional Laplacian bound; it does not mean classical smoothness. A function is a local test function on an open set if it agrees on every relatively compact smaller open set with a global test function. We write $H_g$ for $\operatorname{Hess}g$, and identify the covector $H_g(V,\cdot)$ with a vector when using the notation $H_gV$.

The following facts belong to the standard second-order $\mathrm{RCD}$ calculus (Gigli 2018b, 2019; Savaré 2014) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Gigli2018](#ref-Gigli2018), [Gigli2019](#ref-Gigli2019), [Savare2014](#ref-Savare2014)]. Both test classes are algebras. A test function has an $L^2$ Hessian, and for test fields $$X\in L^2(TM)\cap L^\infty(TM),\qquad
\nabla X\in L^2(T^*M\otimes TM),\qquad
\mathop{\mathrm{div}}X\in L^2(m).$$ The connection is metric-compatible and torsion-free. In particular, inner products of bounded test fields belong to $W^{1,2}$, and their differentials obey the usual product rule. For gradients, $$\nabla_{\nabla g}\nabla g=\nabla\bigl(|\nabla g|^2/2\bigr).$$ The curvature functional <a href="#eq:curvature-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-definition">[eq:curvature-definition]</a> has the algebraic symmetries of Riemannian curvature and is tensorial over $\operatorname{Test}$, including <a id="eq:curvature-symmetries"></a><a id="eq:curvature-tensoriality"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:curvature-symmetries}
R(X,Y,Z,W)&=-R(Y,X,Z,W)=R(Z,W,X,Y),\\
\label{eq:curvature-tensoriality}
R(aX,Y,Z,W)(f)&=R(X,Y,Z,W)(af)
\end{aligned}$$ for test functions $a,f$, with the corresponding identity in every slot (Gigli 2019, Proposition 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Gigli2019](#ref-Gigli2019)].

<a id="stmt-pre-approximation"></a>
<div id="pre:approximation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Cutoffs and approximation). *The following approximations are available.*

1.  *For a compact set $E$ in an open set $U$, there is $\chi\in\operatorname{Test}^\infty$ with $0\le\chi\le1$, equal to one on a neighborhood of $E$, and compactly supported in $U$. There are also exhausting cutoffs $\chi_R\in\operatorname{Test}^\infty$, equal to one on $B_R(o)$, supported in $B_{R+1}(o)$, whose gradient and Laplacian bounds are independent of $R$.*

<a id="eq:pre-hessian-estimate"></a>
2.  *For $g\in D(\Delta)$, $$\label{eq:pre-hessian-estimate}
    \|H_g\|_2^2\le
    \|\Delta g\|_2^2+\max\{-K,0\}\|\nabla g\|_2^2.$$ Compactly supported functions in $\operatorname{Test}^\infty$ form a core for $\Delta$.*

3.  *On a fixed compact region, a function $g\in\operatorname{Test}$ can be approximated by compactly supported $g_j\in\operatorname{Test}^\infty$ with uniformly bounded gradients, with $$g_j\to g,\qquad \nabla g_j\to\nabla g,\qquad
    \Delta g_j\to\Delta g,\qquad H_{g_j}\to H_g
    \quad\hbox{strongly in }L^2$$ on that region.*

4.  *A compactly supported Lipschitz function $\xi$ can be approximated by compactly supported test functions $\xi_j$ with a common compact support and a common Lipschitz bound, uniformly and strongly in $W^{1,2}$. If $\xi\ge0$, the approximants may be chosen nonnegative.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-approximation"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-approximation</summary>

*Proof.* The good-cutoff construction and the integrated Bochner estimate give <span class="upright">(i)</span> and <a href="#eq:pre-hessian-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre-hessian-estimate">[eq:pre-hessian-estimate]</a>; see (Gigli and Tamanini 2021, Appendix A, Lemma A.2 and Equation (A.13)) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[GigliTamanini2021](#ref-GigliTamanini2021)]. For the local version of the cutoff construction one uses finitely many small good cutoffs inside $U$, and a smooth truncation of their sum. This keeps the Laplacian bounded and Sobolev by the test algebra and chain rules.

Let $P_t$ denote the heat semigroup. For $h\in L^2\cap L^\infty$, positive-time regularization gives $P_th\in\operatorname{Test}^\infty$ (Gigli and Tamanini 2021, Appendix A, Equation (A.7)) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[GigliTamanini2021](#ref-GigliTamanini2021)]. Spectral calculus and density of bounded $L^2$ data show that these regularizations form a core. For each such regularization $h$, $$\Delta(\chi_Rh)
=\chi_R\Delta h+h\Delta\chi_R
+2\langle\nabla\chi_R,\nabla h\rangle.$$ The uniform cutoff bounds and the $L^2$ tails imply convergence to $\Delta h$ in $L^2$, as well as convergence of the functions and gradients. This proves the compact-core assertion.

For <span class="upright">(iii)</span>, choose nested cutoffs $\chi,\zeta$ such that $\chi=1$ near the region in question and $\zeta=1$ near $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\chi$. Put $$g_t=\chi P_t(\zeta g).$$ The function $\zeta g$ lies in $\operatorname{Test}$. The product rule, spectral continuity and the fixed cutoff bounds give convergence of $g_t$ to $\chi\zeta g$ in the Laplacian graph norm. The Bakry gradient estimate gives $$\|\nabla g_t\|_\infty
\le \|\chi\|_\infty e^{-Kt}\|\nabla(\zeta g)\|_\infty
+\|\nabla\chi\|_\infty\|\zeta g\|_\infty.$$ Apply <a href="#eq:pre-hessian-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre-hessian-estimate">[eq:pre-hessian-estimate]</a> to $g_t-\chi\zeta g$ to obtain strong Hessian convergence. Since $\chi\zeta g=g$ near the region, <span class="upright">(iii)</span> follows.

For <span class="upright">(iv)</span>, use $\xi_t=\chi P_t\xi$, with an outer cutoff $\chi=1$ near $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\xi$. The heat semigroup converges strongly in $W^{1,2}$; the uniform Lipschitz estimate and the small-time heat-kernel moment bound give uniform convergence on the common compact support. The cutoff product gives the same conclusions for $\xi_t$, and positivity is preserved. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pre-bare-gradients"></a>
<div id="pre:bare-gradients" class="remark">

*Remark 3*. A compactly supported test gradient is a member of the global $\operatorname{TestV}$: multiply it by a test cutoff equal to one near its support. This observation is necessary when $m(M)=\infty$, because the constant function one then need not lie in $\operatorname{Test}$. All uses of a bare test gradient below are local in this sense.

</div>

<a id="stmt-pre-tensor-continuity"></a>
<div id="pre:tensor-continuity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Lipschitz weights and continuity of the tensor). *For fixed test fields the formula <a href="#eq:curvature-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-definition">[eq:curvature-definition]</a> defines a functional on compactly supported Lipschitz weights. It has the form $$f\longmapsto\int fA+\langle\nabla f,B\rangle\,\mathrm dm,
\qquad A\in L^1_{\rm loc}(m),\quad B\in L^2_{\rm loc}(TM).$$ It is continuous under the weight approximation in Lemma <a href="#pre:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:approximation">2</a><span class="upright">(iv)</span>. More generally, for weights with a common compact support, uniform convergence of the weights and weak $L^2$ convergence of their gradients suffice. The tensor identities remain valid for these weights. Moreover, on a fixed compact set the functional is continuous under uniformly bounded field approximations for which the fields, their divergences and their covariant derivatives converge strongly in $L^2$, with the fields converging almost everywhere along a subsequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-tensor-continuity"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-tensor-continuity</summary>

*Proof.* Expand $\nabla_X(fW)=X(f)W+f\nabla_XW$. Every coefficient of $f$ is a product of two $L^2$ factors and bounded factors. The coefficients of $\nabla f$ lie in $L^2$. Uniform convergence of $f_j$ pairs with the fixed $L^1$ coefficient, and weak $L^2$ convergence of their gradients pairs with the fixed $L^2$ coefficient. This proves the weight-continuity assertions. Approximation then extends the identities.

For the field assertion, $$\nabla_{X_j}Y_j-\nabla_XY
=(\nabla Y_j-\nabla Y)(X_j)+\nabla Y(X_j-X).$$ The first term tends to zero in $L^2$ by uniform boundedness of $X_j$; the second does so by dominated convergence. The brackets therefore converge in $L^2$ as well. Products of the resulting strongly convergent $L^2$ quantities converge in $L^1$. Any remaining uniformly bounded field factor is handled by almost-everywhere convergence and domination. This includes the last term of <a href="#eq:curvature-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-definition">[eq:curvature-definition]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Weak Hessians and Bochner measures

<a id="eq:weak-hessian"></a>
For $F\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)$, a compact test gradient $Y=\nabla g$, and a compactly supported Lipschitz weight $\xi$, define $$\label{eq:weak-hessian}
H_F(Y,Y)(\xi)
=-\int_M\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(\xi Y)\,\langle\nabla F,Y\rangle\,\mathrm dm
-\int_M\xi\,\langle\nabla F,\nabla_YY\rangle\,\mathrm dm.$$ Polarization defines the mixed evaluation. The two terms are integrable: $\nabla F$, $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(\xi Y)$ and $\nabla_YY$ are locally $L^2$, and $Y,\xi$ are bounded. For fixed $Y,\xi$, the functional is continuous under weak local $W^{1,2}$ convergence of $F$. When $F\in D(\Delta)$, this agrees with the usual $L^2$ Hessian. A statement $H_F(Y,Y)\le A\,m$ means the corresponding inequality on every nonnegative compact Lipschitz weight. It does not presuppose that $H_F$ is an $L^2$ tensor.

<a id="eq:bochner-hessian"></a>
<a id="eq:bochner-dimension"></a>
For a local test function $g$, the measure-valued Bochner inequalities are $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:bochner-hessian}
\Gamma_2(g)
&:=\Delta\bigl(|\nabla g|^2/2\bigr)
-\langle\nabla g,\nabla\Delta g\rangle m
\ge \bigl(K|\nabla g|^2+|H_g|^2\bigr)m,\\
\label{eq:bochner-dimension}
\Gamma_2(g)&\ge
\bigl(K|\nabla g|^2+(\Delta g)^2/N\bigr)m.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the first Laplacian is a measure-valued Laplacian. We use the two inequalities separately or after taking a convex combination; no inequality with the sum of both squared terms and coefficient one is asserted. Our capacity normalization is $$\operatorname{Cap}_2(E)=
\inf_{\substack{U\supset E\\U\ \mathrm{open}}}
\ \inf_{\substack{v\in W^{1,2}(M)\\v\ge1\ m\text{-a.e.\ on }U}}
\int_M\bigl(v^2+|\nabla v|^2\bigr)\,\mathrm dm.$$ A polar set has zero $2$-capacity; *quasi-everywhere* means outside a polar set. The Bochner measures do not charge sets of zero $2$-capacity. Their Sobolev multipliers are evaluated using quasi-continuous representatives (Savaré 2014, sec. 2.3 and 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Savare2014](#ref-Savare2014)].

More explicitly, if bounded Sobolev multipliers converge strongly in $W^{1,2}$, with a common bound and compact support, a subsequence converges outside a polar set. Dominated convergence therefore applies to their integrals against each fixed local Bochner measure. This extends the integration-by-parts identities from test multipliers to bounded Sobolev multipliers. The same argument shows that an almost-everywhere lower bound for a Sobolev multiplier holds for its quasi-continuous representative outside a polar set. These facts will be used when multiplying singular Bochner measures in Section <a href="#sec:heat-index" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat-index">4</a>.

The inequalities <a href="#eq:bochner-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bochner-hessian">[eq:bochner-hessian]</a>–<a href="#eq:bochner-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bochner-dimension">[eq:bochner-dimension]</a> also hold for our original test class, without a bounded Laplacian. One can see the extension locally by applying heat regularization to a test function. The gradients remain uniformly bounded, the Hessians converge strongly in $L^2$, and $\nabla\Delta P_tg\to\nabla\Delta g$ in $L^2$. The local masses of the positive Bochner remainders are bounded by testing with a fixed outer cutoff. Passage to the distributional limit gives the asserted measure inequalities.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Geometry, transport and along-curve calculus

We use the following established facts, stating the restrictions that are relevant to the proof.

<a id="eq:local-volume"></a>
1.  A finite-dimensional full-support $\mathrm{RCD}$ space is proper and geodesic, is locally doubling, and satisfies a local $L^2$ Poincaré inequality and the Sobolev-to-Lipschitz property. Bounded sets have finite measure. When the dimension parameter is $N=n$ and $m=\mathcal H^n$, almost every point is $n$-regular and the tangent module has dimension $n$ almost everywhere. For every bounded region and every fixed sufficiently small upper radius, $$\label{eq:local-volume}
    c\,r^n\le m(B_r(x))\le C\,r^n$$ with positive constants uniform for centers in that region. The upper bound is the noncollapsed density bound; the lower bound follows from Bishop–Gromov and full support (De Philippis and Gigli 2018, Theorem 1.12 and Corollary 2.14) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[DPG2018](#ref-DPG2018)].

2.  Optimal quadratic transport between probability measures $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal P_2(M)$, with $\mu\ll m$, is induced by a unique dynamical map. For a fixed real-valued $c$-concave potential, $c=d^2/2$, the geodesic from almost every point into its $c$-superdifferential is unique (Gigli, Rajala, and Sturm 2016, Theorems 1.1 and 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[GigliRajalaSturm2016](#ref-GigliRajalaSturm2016)]. Between bounded densities with bounded support, the interpolating densities are uniformly bounded and obey the pointwise curvature-dimension distortion inequality. We use the density bound also on subintervals with a singular endpoint, whenever a separate positive-time compression estimate has been proved. We do not assume bounded compression at a Dirac endpoint.

3.  A bounded-speed plan with bounded compression is a Sobolev test plan. For a Sobolev function $g$, its time derivative is $\langle\nabla g,v\rangle$, where $v$ is the plan velocity (Gigli 2018a, Theorem/Definition 2.33) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[GigliDifferentialStructure](#ref-GigliDifferentialStructure)]. For a $W_2$-continuous curve with bounded compression, the Sobolev continuity equation characterizes $2$-absolute continuity and its action, and such curves have a superposition by absolutely continuous metric paths (Gigli and Han 2015, Theorem 3.5 and Proposition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[GigliHan2015](#ref-GigliHan2015)]. The required $W_2$-continuity is verified before using this criterion in Section <a href="#sec:heat-index" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat-index">4</a>.

4.  For a bounded-support optimal geodesic test plan, Gigli–Tamanini’s second-order differentiation formula applies to $H^{2,2}$ functions and to vector-field pairings with $H_C^{1,2}(TM)$, the closure of $\operatorname{TestV}(M)$ in the covariant Sobolev norm (Gigli and Tamanini 2021, Theorem 5.13) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[GigliTamanini2021](#ref-GigliTamanini2021)]. Every vector field to which we apply this theorem is a compactly supported test field. It also applies after bounded measurable restriction of the plan. This permits passage from integrated identities to almost-sure time-distributional identities.

5.  A compactly supported time-dependent test vector field, bounded with bounded divergence and integrable Sobolev covariant derivative, has the regular Lagrangian flow and uniqueness properties used for the short flows below (Ambrosio and Trevisan 2014, sec. 8 and 9.6) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[AmbrosioTrevisan2014](#ref-AmbrosioTrevisan2014)]. The compression estimate is the exponential of the integrated negative-divergence bound. The corresponding backward flow gives the inverse estimate. These are Sobolev flows; no spatial Lipschitz bound on the vector field is assumed.

The more specialized harmonic splitting, tangent-continuity, heat-kernel and variable-curvature theorems are stated at their points of use. For the geometric implication from <span class="upright">(A)</span> to the metric-measure part of <span class="upright">(B)</span>, we use the Alexandrov curvature-dimension and infinitesimal Hilbertianity results (Petrunin 2011; Zhang and Zhu 2010; Kuwae, Machigashira, and Shioya 2001) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Petrunin2011](#ref-Petrunin2011), [ZhangZhu2010](#ref-ZhangZhu2010), [KMS2001](#ref-KMS2001)]. The unreduced statement for all curvature bounds, including Alexandrov spaces with boundary, is recorded in (Kapovitch, Ketterer, and Sturm 2023, Theorem 2.9 and Corollary 2.10) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[KapovitchKettererSturm](#ref-KapovitchKettererSturm)]. For the converse we use unreduced $\mathrm{CD}$ as a hypothesis; invoking analytic results written with $\mathrm{RCD}^*$ consequently changes neither $K$ nor $N$.

<a id="sec:forward"></a>
### From Alexandrov comparison to the tensor bound

<a id="stmt-fwd-curvature"></a>
<div id="fwd:curvature" class="theorem">

<a id="fwd:target"></a>
**Theorem 5**. *Let $(M,d)$ be a complete $n$-dimensional Alexandrov space with curvature bounded below by $\kappa$, where $n\geq2$, and put $m=\mathcal H^n_d$. Then $(M,d,m)$ is $\mathrm{RCD}((n-1)\kappa,n)$, its measure has full support, and $$\label{fwd:target}
 R(X,Y,Y,X)(f)
 \geq \kappa\int f\bigl(|X|^2|Y|^2-\langle X,Y\rangle^2\bigr)\,\mathrm dm$$ for every nonnegative $f\in\operatorname{Test}(M)$ and every $X,Y\in\operatorname{TestV}(M)$, with the global test classes and curvature convention of Theorem <a href="#main:equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:equivalence">1</a>.*

</div>

The curvature-dimension and full-support assertions follow from the Alexandrov-to-$\mathrm{CD}$ theorem and infinitesimal Hilbertianity (Petrunin 2011; Zhang and Zhu 2010; Kuwae, Machigashira, and Shioya 2001) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Petrunin2011](#ref-Petrunin2011), [ZhangZhu2010](#ref-ZhangZhu2010), [KMS2001](#ref-KMS2001)]. The unreduced statement for arbitrary $\kappa$, including complete Alexandrov spaces with boundary, is recorded directly in (Kapovitch, Ketterer, and Sturm 2023, Theorem 2.9 and Corollary 2.10) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[KapovitchKettererSturm](#ref-KapovitchKettererSturm)]; it requires only local finiteness of $\mathcal H^n$, with no compactness or assumption of finite total mass. Set $K=(n-1)\kappa$. We shall use the resulting RCD calculus throughout this section. In particular, all covariant derivatives below are weak covariant derivatives. To prove the tensor inequality, we compare two energies associated with short flows in finitely many gradient directions: the action of the radial flows and the optimal transport action between their endpoint measures. Alexandrov comparison bounds twice the averaged radial action minus the averaged pair action from below. For the upper bound, a dual transport calculation first produces an error measuring radial acceleration. Second-order endpoint corrections remove that error, after which averaging identifies the upper bound as curvature plus a nonnegative square. Sobolev dual potentials chosen separately on finitely many localization pieces make this remaining square arbitrarily small. Positivity and tensoriality then extend the resulting gradient inequality to test coefficients; local approximation and exhaustion recover the global test classes. The successive limits are specified in Remark <a href="#fwd:limit-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fwd:limit-order">17</a>.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Conventions and a short-flow expansion

All unlabelled spatial integrals in this section are with respect to $m$. Write $$|U\wedge V|^2:=|U|^2|V|^2-\langle U,V\rangle^2.$$ $$\mathcal G:=\{\nabla u:
 u\in\operatorname{Test}(M),\ \operatorname{supp}u\text{ compact},\
 \Delta u\in L^\infty(M)\}.$$ Every member of $\mathcal G$ is bounded, has bounded divergence, and has covariant derivative in $L^2$. It belongs to the global $\operatorname{TestV}(M)$ by Remark <a href="#pre:bare-gradients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:bare-gradients">3</a>. Throughout we use the stronger test cutoffs of Lemma <a href="#pre:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:approximation">2</a><span class="upright">(i)</span>.

For compactly supported Lipschitz $f$, we use $R(X,Y,Z,W)(f)$ to denote the same integrated expression as in the statement, even when $f\notin\operatorname{Test}(M)$. Lemma <a href="#pre:tensor-continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:tensor-continuity">4</a> gives this extension, its tensor identities, and continuity for weights with common compact support that converge uniformly with weakly convergent $L^2$ gradients.

<a id="fwd:centering"></a>
We choose a finite probability law on compact test potentials with bounded Laplacian, invariant under $u\mapsto-u$. Let $A=\nabla u_A$ and $B=\nabla u_B$ be independent choices from this law. The notation $\mathbb E$ always refers to this finite averaging. The example to keep in mind is the uniform law giving the four fields $U,-U,V,-V$, with $U,V\in\mathcal G$. Sign symmetry will cancel the odd terms in the energy expansion, while the mixed pairs retain the sectional-curvature expression for $U,V$. We work with a general finite law because the calculation uses only independence and sign symmetry. In particular, $$\label{fwd:centering}
 \mathbb E u_A=0,\qquad \mathbb E A=0,\qquad
 \mathbb E|A-B|^2=2\mathbb E|A|^2.$$ For a fixed compactly supported Lipschitz function $f\geq0$, and a constant linear combination $C$ of the chosen gradients, put $$L_C:=-\operatorname{div}(fC)
      =-\langle\nabla f,C\rangle-f\operatorname{div}C.$$ This is a bounded function with compact support. The case $\int f=0$ is trivial, so we may assume that $\int f>0$.

<a id="fwd:prescribed-velocity"></a>
For each choice of $A$, let $S_A$ be a bounded compact test field with bounded divergence. These fields provide the second-order endpoint corrections: the acceleration of the leading gradient flow is $\nabla_AA$, so they will approach $-\nabla_AA$ only after the limit in $h$. For $0<h<1$, let $F^h_{r,A}$, $0\leq r\leq1$, be the regular Lagrangian flow of $$\label{fwd:prescribed-velocity}
 b^h_{r,A}=hA+h^2rS_A.$$ Denote its transported density and endpoint measure by $$(F^h_{r,A})_\#(fm)=\rho^h_{r,A}m,
 \qquad \rho^h_A:=\rho^h_{1,A},\qquad \mu^h_A:=\rho^h_A m.$$ All statements as $h\downarrow0$ initially keep the finite family, the weight, and the fields $S_A$ fixed.

<a id="stmt-fwd-flow"></a>
<div id="fwd:flow" class="lemma">

<a id="fwd:flow-density-bound"></a>
<a id="fwd:flow-first"></a>
<a id="fwd:flow-second"></a>
<a id="fwd:increment"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Short-flow density expansion). *The flows above exist and are invertible modulo null sets. Their forward and inverse compression constants are $1+O(h)$, their displacements are $O(h)$, and their transported densities have support in a common compact set. Uniformly in $r\in[0,1]$, $$\label{fwd:flow-density-bound}
 \|\rho^h_{r,A}-f\|_{L^\infty}=O(h).$$ Moreover, $$\label{fwd:flow-first}
 \frac{\rho^h_{r,A}-f}{h}\rightharpoonup^* rL_A$$ in $L^\infty$ on the common bounded support, both at each fixed $r$ and on $[0,1]\times M$. For every fixed $q\in W^{1,2}(M)$, $$\label{fwd:flow-second}
 \int q\rho^h_A
 =\int qf+h\int qL_A
 +\frac{h^2}{2}\int
   \langle\nabla q,fS_A+L_AA\rangle+o(h^2).$$ If $g$ is Lipschitz on a neighborhood of the common support, then $$\label{fwd:increment}
 \frac{g(F^h_{1,A}(z))-g(z)}{h}
 \longrightarrow dg(A)(z)
 \quad\text{in }L^1(fm).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fwd-flow"><summary>Proof of stmt-fwd-flow</summary>

*Proof.* The fields in <a href="#fwd:prescribed-velocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:prescribed-velocity">[fwd:prescribed-velocity]</a> are bounded, have $L^2$ covariant derivative and bounded divergence, and have support in a fixed compact set. The Sobolev flow theorem applies (Ambrosio and Trevisan 2014, Theorems 4.3, 5.4, 6.7 and 8.3; Section 9.6) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[AmbrosioTrevisan2014](#ref-AmbrosioTrevisan2014)]. Applying it also to the time-reversed field, and using uniqueness, gives the inverse flow. The divergence bound is $O(h)$, so both compression constants are $1+O(h)$. The speed is bounded by $h\|A\|_\infty+h^2\|S_A\|_\infty$, which gives the displacement and support assertions. Properness is used here to replace bounded closed neighborhoods by compact sets.

To check <a href="#fwd:flow-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-density-bound">[fwd:flow-density-bound]</a>, the pushforward of $m$ under the flow has density between $1-O(h)$ and $1+O(h)$, by the forward and inverse compression bounds. Replacing $f(z)$ by $f(F^h_{r,A}(z))$ in the pushforward changes the density by at most $O(h)\operatorname{Lip}(f)$, again by compression and the displacement bound. Multiplication of the pushed-forward reference density by $f$ changes it from $f$ by at most $O(h)\|f\|_\infty$. This proves <a href="#fwd:flow-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-density-bound">[fwd:flow-density-bound]</a> without requiring a spatially Lipschitz velocity field.

<a id="fwd:flow-ce"></a>
The continuity equation, tested with $q\in W^{1,2}$, reads $$\label{fwd:flow-ce}
 \int q(\rho^h_{r,A}-f)
 =\int_0^r\int
   \langle\nabla q,hA+h^2tS_A\rangle\rho^h_{t,A}\,\mathrm dt.$$ All pairings are finite because the densities are bounded and have bounded support. Divide by $h$ and use <a href="#fwd:flow-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-density-bound">[fwd:flow-density-bound]</a>. The right-hand side converges, uniformly in $r$, to $r\int f\langle\nabla q,A\rangle=r\int qL_A$. The uniform $L^\infty$ bound on the left quotient, followed by density of compact Lipschitz functions in $L^1$ on bounded sets, proves <a href="#fwd:flow-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-first">[fwd:flow-first]</a>, also in space-time. Use <a href="#fwd:flow-ce" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-ce">[fwd:flow-ce]</a> once more, with $r=1$, to obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \int q(\rho^h_A-f)
 ={}&h\int f\langle\nabla q,A\rangle
 +h^2\int_0^1\int
   \left\langle\nabla q,
    \frac{\rho^h_{r,A}-f}{h}A+r\rho^h_{r,A}S_A\right\rangle\,\mathrm dr.
\end{aligned}$$ The functions paired with the normalized density difference are fixed and integrable on its support. Equation <a href="#fwd:flow-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-first">[fwd:flow-first]</a> therefore gives <a href="#fwd:flow-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-second">[fwd:flow-second]</a>.

For completeness, if $v\in L^1$ on a common bounded neighborhood, then $$v\circ F^h_{r,A}\longrightarrow v
 \quad\text{in }L^1(\,\mathrm dr\,fm).$$ Indeed this holds for continuous functions by the displacement bound, and follows for $L^1$ functions by approximation and the uniform compression bound. Differentiation of a Lipschitz function along the flow gives $$\frac{g(F^h_{1,A}(z))-g(z)}h
 =\int_0^1 dg(A)(F^h_{r,A}(z))\,\mathrm dr
  +h\int_0^1r\,dg(S_A)(F^h_{r,A}(z))\,\mathrm dr.$$ The preceding composition convergence proves <a href="#fwd:increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:increment">[fwd:increment]</a>. A cutoff may be inserted when $g$ is only locally Lipschitz. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The configuration comparison

If $\mu,\nu$ are finite nonnegative measures of equal mass, define their transport energy by $$J(\mu,\nu):=\frac12
 \inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int d(x,y)^2\,\mathrm d\gamma(x,y).$$ This is the usual half squared Wasserstein distance after probability normalization, multiplied by the common mass. Let $$J^h_{AB}:=J(\mu^h_A,\mu^h_B),\qquad
 J^h_{0A}:=\frac12\int_0^1\int
       |hA+h^2rS_A|^2\rho^h_{r,A}\,\mathrm dr.$$ The second quantity is the energy of the prescribed radial flow, not its optimal transport energy.

<a id="stmt-fwd-configuration"></a>
<div id="fwd:configuration" class="lemma">

<a id="fwd:distance-first"></a>
<a id="fwd:energy-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Fourth-order configuration inequality). *For the above centered finite law and flows, put $$a^h_A(z):=d(z,F^h_{1,A}(z)),\qquad
 e^h_{AB}(z):=d(F^h_{1,A}(z),F^h_{1,B}(z)).$$ Then $$\label{fwd:distance-first}
 \frac{a^h_A}{h}\longrightarrow |A|,\qquad
 \frac{e^h_{AB}}h\longrightarrow |A-B|$$ in measure and in every finite $L^p(fm)$, for each choice of $A,B$. Furthermore, $$\label{fwd:energy-comparison}
 \liminf_{h\downarrow0}
 \frac{2\mathbb E J^h_{0A}-\mathbb E J^h_{AB}}{h^4}
 \geq\frac\kappa6\int f\,\mathbb E|A\wedge B|^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fwd-configuration"><summary>Proof of stmt-fwd-configuration</summary>

<a id="fwd:cosine-sum"></a>
*Proof.* At $m$-almost every base point $z$, the Alexandrov tangent cone is Euclidean. Choose a geodesic from $z$ to each endpoint $F^h_{1,A}(z)$. For the finite weights $p_A$, the radial vectors $v_A\in T_zM$ satisfy $$\sum_{A,B}p_Ap_B|v_A||v_B|\cos\angle(v_A,v_B)
 =\left|\sum_Ap_Av_A\right|^2\geq0.$$ Actual angles are at least the corresponding $\kappa$-comparison angles. Consequently, $$\label{fwd:cosine-sum}
 \mathbb E\!\left[
 a^h_Aa^h_B\cos\widetilde\angle_\kappa
          (a^h_A,a^h_B,e^h_{AB})\right]\geq0.$$ Zero radial lengths contribute zero. All sides are $O(h)$, so the model triangles are defined for sufficiently small $h$, including when $\kappa>0$.

<a id="fwd:model-expansion"></a>
For radial side lengths $a,b$ and opposite side length $e$, put $z_0=(a^2+b^2-e^2)/2$. The model cosine law gives, uniformly for such small triangles, $$\label{fwd:model-expansion}
 ab\cos\widetilde\angle_\kappa
 =z_0-\frac\kappa6(a^2b^2-z_0^2)+O(h^6).$$ For $\kappa=0$ this is exact without the correction. For $\kappa\ne0$, substitute the Taylor series of the model sine and cosine into their cosine law. After multiplication by $ab$, the denominators are the nonvanishing analytic factors $\mathfrak s_\kappa(a)/a$ and $\mathfrak s_\kappa(b)/b$, where $\mathfrak s_\kappa''+\kappa\mathfrak s_\kappa=0$, $\mathfrak s_\kappa(0)=0$, and $\mathfrak s_\kappa'(0)=1$. This also proves uniformity at degenerate triangles and zero sides.

We first identify the leading distances. By integration of the speed and the composition convergence used in Lemma <a href="#fwd:flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fwd:flow">6</a>, $$\left(\frac{a^h_A}{h}-|A|\right)_+
 \longrightarrow0\quad\text{in }L^1(fm).$$ There is a countable collection of scalar $1$-Lipschitz functions whose differentials norm every tangent vector almost everywhere; one may use the norming family in (Ambrosio and Trevisan 2014, Lemma 9.2) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[AmbrosioTrevisan2014](#ref-AmbrosioTrevisan2014)], with local cutoffs when needed. For each member $g$, <a href="#fwd:increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:increment">[fwd:increment]</a> and its difference for two flows give the lower bounds $$a^h_A/h\geq
 |g(F^h_{1,A})-g|/h,\qquad
 e^h_{AB}/h\geq
 |g(F^h_{1,A})-g(F^h_{1,B})|/h.$$ First taking a finite part of the norming family, and then increasing it, shows that the negative parts of $a^h_A/h-|A|$ and $e^h_{AB}/h-|A-B|$ tend to zero in measure. The radial convergence follows. All scaled distances are uniformly bounded, so this convergence also holds in every finite $L^p(fm)$.

Equations <a href="#fwd:cosine-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:cosine-sum">[fwd:cosine-sum]</a>–<a href="#fwd:model-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:model-expansion">[fwd:model-expansion]</a>, initially retaining only their quadratic terms, imply $$\mathbb E\int f(e^h_{AB}/h)^2
 \leq 2\mathbb E\int f(a^h_A/h)^2+O(h^2)
 \longrightarrow 2\mathbb E\int f|A|^2.$$ By <a href="#fwd:centering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:centering">[fwd:centering]</a>, the last quantity is $\mathbb E\int f|A-B|^2$. The pair-distance lower bounds already proved imply convergence of the negative errors in $L^1$. The displayed upper bound therefore forces the positive errors to converge in $L^1$ as well. Since the family is finite, this proves the pair convergence in <a href="#fwd:distance-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:distance-first">[fwd:distance-first]</a> for every pair of positive probability.

<a id="fwd:distance-fourth"></a>
We may now retain the quartic term in <a href="#fwd:model-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:model-expansion">[fwd:model-expansion]</a>. The convergence just proved gives $$h^{-4}\left((a^h_Aa^h_B)^2
 -\left(\frac{(a^h_A)^2+(a^h_B)^2-(e^h_{AB})^2}{2}\right)^2\right)
 \longrightarrow |A\wedge B|^2$$ in $L^1(fm)$. Thus $$\label{fwd:distance-fourth}
 \liminf_{h\downarrow0}\frac1{h^4}
 \left(\mathbb E\int f(a^h_A)^2
       -\frac12\mathbb E\int f(e^h_{AB})^2\right)
 \geq\frac\kappa6\int f\,\mathbb E|A\wedge B|^2.$$ The radial energy dominates half the squared radial displacement, and $(F^h_{1,A},F^h_{1,B})_\#(fm)$ is a coupling of the endpoint measures. Hence $$2J^h_{0A}\geq\int f(a^h_A)^2,
 \qquad J^h_{AB}\leq\frac12\int f(e^h_{AB})^2.$$ Their combination with <a href="#fwd:distance-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:distance-fourth">[fwd:distance-fourth]</a> proves <a href="#fwd:energy-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:energy-comparison">[fwd:energy-comparison]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### First variation of the optimal interpolation densities

The transport expansion will pair the first density variation with fixed $L^2$ Hessian coefficients. We therefore need an $O(h)$ bound in $L^2$, as well as identification of the weak limit. For the next lemma, assume that $f$ is semiconcave with some finite constant along geodesics in a bounded region containing all the interpolations under consideration. No bound for that constant is required uniformly over later approximations of $f$. Let $\sigma^h_s m$, $0\leq s\leq1$, be the optimal interpolation from $\mu^h_A$ to $\mu^h_B$, and let $v^h_s$ be its minimal continuity-equation velocity. Thus $$2J^h_{AB}=\int_0^1\int\sigma^h_s|v^h_s|^2\,\mathrm ds.$$ Existence, uniqueness, and bounded densities are used in their usual finite-dimensional RCD form (Gigli, Rajala, and Sturm 2016; Rajala 2013) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[GigliRajalaSturm2016](#ref-GigliRajalaSturm2016), [Rajala2012](#ref-Rajala2012)]. We derive the needed pointwise distortion inequality from integrated $\mathrm{CD}$ by restriction below.

<a id="stmt-fwd-density"></a>
<div id="fwd:density" class="lemma">

<a id="fwd:density-bound"></a>
<a id="fwd:velocity-first"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Optimal density first variation). *Put $D=B-A$ and $C_s=(1-s)A+sB$. The densities $\sigma^h_s$ have common compact support and a uniform $L^\infty$ bound. They satisfy $$\label{fwd:density-bound}
 \sup_{0\leq s\leq1}\|\sigma^h_s-f\|_{L^2}^2=O(h^2),
 \qquad
 \frac{\sigma^h_s-f}{h}\rightharpoonup L_{C_s}
 \quad\text{in }L^2(\,\mathrm ds\,\mathrm dm).$$ Their velocities satisfy $$\label{fwd:velocity-first}
 \int_0^1\int\sigma^h_s|v^h_s/h-D|^2\,\mathrm ds\longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fwd-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-fwd-density</summary>

*Proof.* *Restriction of the plan.* The endpoints have uniformly bounded densities and support in a common ball. All connecting geodesics lie in a larger fixed ball, which is compact. The standard interpolation bound gives the uniform density estimate. We record a sharper consequence needed below. Weaken the Ricci parameter to a fixed $K'<0$. The zero-mass case is immediate; otherwise let $\pi^h$ be the optimal dynamical plan and normalize it to a probability $\pi$, with marginals $r_t m$. By (Gigli, Rajala, and Sturm 2016, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[GigliRajalaSturm2016](#ref-GigliRajalaSturm2016)], $\pi$ has a Borel conull set on which every evaluation $e_t$, $t<1$, is injective. Both endpoints are absolutely continuous. Applying the same theorem to the reversed plan, pulling its conull set back under time reversal, and intersecting with the first set gives a Borel conull $\Gamma$ on which $e_t$ is injective for every $t\in[0,1]$.

Fix $s\in(0,1)$, and let $H\geq0$ be bounded Borel on $\Gamma$, with $Z=\int H\,\mathrm d\pi>0$. The probability $\pi_H=(H/Z)\pi$ is optimal for its endpoint marginals: after scaling $H\leq1$, a cheaper transport for $H\pi$ could be spliced with $\pi-H\pi$, contradicting optimality. Its endpoint densities are bounded and absolutely continuous. The integrated $\mathrm{CD}(K',n)$ inequality supplies an optimal dynamical plan for these marginals, and uniqueness forces that plan to be $\pi_H$. The measurable inverses of $e_t|_\Gamma$ give, by Lusin–Souslin, $$h_t=H\circ(e_t|_\Gamma)^{-1},\qquad
 r_t^H=(h_t/Z)r_t,\qquad t\in\{0,s,1\},$$ where $h_t$ is extended by zero off $e_t\Gamma$. Write $\beta_t(\ell)=\tau_{K',n}^{(t)}(\ell)$ for the unreduced distortion coefficient and set $$F_s(\gamma)=r_s(\gamma_s)^{-1/n}
 -\beta_{1-s}(\ell)r_0(\gamma_0)^{-1/n}
 -\beta_s(\ell)r_1(\gamma_1)^{-1/n}.$$ The integrated inequality for $\pi_H$, after cancellation of $Z^{1/n-1}$, is exactly $$\int H^{1-1/n}F_s\,\mathrm d\pi\geq0.$$ There are no undefined inverse powers in this calculation: zero or infinite density values lie over marginal-null sets at the three fixed times, and on the common finite-measure compact support $Q$, $$\int r_t(\gamma_t)^{-1/n}\,\mathrm d\pi
 =\int_Q r_t^{1-1/n}\,\mathrm dm<\infty.$$ Taking $H$ to be indicators of Borel sets proves $F_s\geq0$ $\pi$-almost everywhere. Restoring the common mass preserves this homogeneous inequality. Its exceptional set may depend on $s$; every fixed-time estimate below has the same constants, which suffices for the supremum over $s$. Jointly Borel density versions and Fubini give the corresponding $\,\mathrm ds\otimes\pi$-almost-everywhere statements for time integrals.

<a id="fwd:pointwise-density"></a>
*The density norm bound.* On the bounded range of possible geodesic lengths $\ell$, the pointwise CD distortion coefficients, divided by their zero-curvature coefficients, have a positive lower bound and are at least $1-C\ell^2$, uniformly in $s$. The negative power mean is at most the arithmetic mean. Since the endpoint densities are bounded, the pointwise CD inequality therefore implies, along the optimal dynamical plan $\pi^h$, $$\label{fwd:pointwise-density}
 \sigma^h_s(\gamma_s)
 \leq (1-s)\rho^h_A(\gamma_0)
       +s\rho^h_B(\gamma_1)+C\ell(\gamma)^2.$$ To see that the error remains valid when $\ell$ is not small, let $a(\ell)$ be a common positive lower bound for the two distortion ratios. The CD inequality first gives the negative power mean multiplied by $a(\ell)^{-n}$. On the fixed length interval, $a(\ell)^{-n}-1\leq C\ell^2$. This proves <a href="#fwd:pointwise-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:pointwise-density">[fwd:pointwise-density]</a>, including such lengths.

Integration against $\pi^h$ gives $$\int(\sigma^h_s)^2
 \leq(1-s)\int(\rho^h_A)^2+s\int(\rho^h_B)^2
      +C\int\ell^2\,\mathrm d\pi^h.$$ Semiconcavity of $f$ gives $$-2\int f\sigma^h_s
 \leq-2(1-s)\int f\rho^h_A-2s\int f\rho^h_B
      +C\int\ell^2\,\mathrm d\pi^h.$$ Here $\int\ell^2\,\mathrm d\pi^h=2J^h_{AB}=O(h^2)$, by the configuration coupling. Adding the last two inequalities and $\int f^2$ proves the norm bound in <a href="#fwd:density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:density-bound">[fwd:density-bound]</a>, using <a href="#fwd:flow-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-density-bound">[fwd:flow-density-bound]</a> for the endpoints.

*First variations of velocity and density.* The same coupling and <a href="#fwd:distance-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:distance-first">[fwd:distance-first]</a> imply $$\limsup_{h\downarrow0}\int_0^1\int
     \sigma^h_s|v^h_s/h|^2\,\mathrm ds\leq\int f|D|^2.$$ The continuity equation tested with $u_B-u_A$, and then <a href="#fwd:flow-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-second">[fwd:flow-second]</a>, gives $$\int_0^1\int\sigma^h_s\langle v^h_s/h,D\rangle\,\mathrm ds
 =\frac1h\int(u_B-u_A)(\rho^h_B-\rho^h_A)
 \longrightarrow\int f|D|^2.$$ The norm estimate for the densities also gives $\int_0^1\int\sigma^h_s|D|^2\,\mathrm ds\to\int f|D|^2$. Expanding the square proves <a href="#fwd:velocity-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:velocity-first">[fwd:velocity-first]</a>.

Finally, for any fixed $q\in W^{1,2}(M)$, $$\int q\frac{\sigma^h_s-f}{h}
 =\int q\frac{\rho^h_A-f}{h}
   +\int_0^s\int\sigma^h_t\langle\nabla q,v^h_t/h\rangle\,\mathrm dt.$$ The first term converges to $\int qL_A$. In the second, use <a href="#fwd:velocity-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:velocity-first">[fwd:velocity-first]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz to replace $v^h_t/h$ by $D$; then use the density norm bound to replace $\sigma^h_t$ by $f$. The error tends to zero uniformly in $s$. The limit is $s\int f\langle\nabla q,D\rangle
=s\int qL_D$. Boundedness of the normalized density differences in $L^2(\,\mathrm ds\,\mathrm dm)$, and density of finite sums of separated space-time test functions, prove the weak convergence in <a href="#fwd:density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:density-bound">[fwd:density-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The fourth-order transport estimate

The configuration comparison gives a lower bound for $2\mathbb E J^h_{0A}-\mathbb E J^h_{AB}$. We now obtain an upper bound for the same difference in terms of curvature and a nonnegative square, which the following subsection removes by localization.

Continue to assume that $f$ is semiconcave as above, keeping the successive limits of Remark <a href="#fwd:limit-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fwd:limit-order">17</a>. For each ordered pair $A,B$, choose an arbitrary fixed $q_{AB}\in W^{1,2}(M)$. The choices need not have any symmetry. These are dual energy tests. After extending the error estimate to Lipschitz weights, we will choose their gradients on finitely many localization pieces to approximate $-\nabla_A(B-A)$ and remove the remaining square. Each potential is held fixed during the short-flow limit. For the duration of a calculation with this pair write $$D=B-A,\qquad C_s=(1-s)A+sB,\qquad T_D=\nabla_DD,$$ $$q_s=q_{AB}-s\frac{|D|^2}{2},\qquad
 Q_s=\nabla q_s=\nabla q_{AB}-sT_D.$$ The identity for $Q_s$ follows from Hessian symmetry, since $D$ is a gradient. Notice that $T_D\in L^2$ and that $|D|^2/2\in W^{1,2}$. No Hessian of $q_{AB}$ is required.

<a id="stmt-fwd-dual"></a>
<div id="fwd:dual" class="lemma">

<a id="fwd:dual-expansion"></a>
<a id="fwd:dual-coefficient"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Dual expansion and radial defect). *With the preceding notation, $$\begin{aligned}
 J^h_{AB}\geq{}&
 h\int(u_B-u_A)(\rho^h_B-\rho^h_A)
 -\frac{h^2}{2}\int f|D|^2
 -h^3\int_0^1\int f\langle C_s,T_D\rangle\,\mathrm ds
 \nonumber\\
 &\quad+h^4K_{AB}(S)+o(h^4),
 \label{fwd:dual-expansion}\\
 K_{AB}(S):={}&
 \frac12\int\left[
  \langle Q_1,fS_B+L_BB\rangle
  -\langle Q_0,fS_A+L_AA\rangle\right]
 \nonumber\\
 &\quad-\int_0^1\int
     \left[L_{C_s}\langle D,Q_s\rangle+\frac f2|Q_s|^2\right]\,\mathrm ds.
 \label{fwd:dual-coefficient}
\end{aligned}$$ For the prescribed radial action $J^h_{0A}$, take pair indices $0,A$, zero initial potential and fields, $\rho^h_0=f$, and $q_{0A}=0$. Then <a href="#fwd:dual-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:dual-expansion">[fwd:dual-expansion]</a> holds with equality and fourth-order coefficient <a id="fwd:radial-defect"></a>$$\label{fwd:radial-defect}
 K_{0A}(S)+\frac16\int f|S_A+\nabla_AA|^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fwd-dual"><summary>Proof of stmt-fwd-dual</summary>

<a id="fwd:young-identity"></a>
*Proof.* *The energy identity.* For a bounded-compression solution $\sigma_s$ of the continuity equation with velocity $v_s$, use $$p_s=h(u_B-u_A)+h^2q_s.$$ Its strong $W^{1,2}$ dependence on time and the continuity equation give the exact energy identity $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac12\int_0^1\int\sigma_s|v_s|^2\,\mathrm ds
 ={}&\left[\int p_s\sigma_s\right]_{s=0}^{s=1}
 -\int_0^1\int\sigma_s
      \left(\partial_sp_s+\frac12|\nabla p_s|^2\right)\,\mathrm ds
 \nonumber\\
 &\quad+\frac12\int_0^1\int\sigma_s|v_s-\nabla p_s|^2\,\mathrm ds.
 \label{fwd:young-identity}
\end{aligned}$$ One may verify the time differentiation directly by writing $p_s$ as a sum of fixed Sobolev functions with affine time coefficients. The endpoint terms are finite by the bounded densities and bounded support; the remaining terms are finite by the kinetic-energy and $W^{1,2}$ bounds. Thus no boundedness assumption on $q_{AB}$ is needed.

*The dual expansion.* The term $-s|D|^2/2$ in $q_s$ cancels the quadratic part of the Hamilton–Jacobi residual. Thus the residual begins at order $h^3$, and the first density variation supplies its contribution at order $h^4$. Explicitly, <a id="fwd:residual"></a>$$\label{fwd:residual}
 \partial_sp_s+\frac12|\nabla p_s|^2
 =h^3\langle D,Q_s\rangle+\frac{h^4}{2}|Q_s|^2.$$ For the optimal interpolation, Lemma <a href="#fwd:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fwd:density">8</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_0^1\int\sigma^h_s\langle D,Q_s\rangle\,\mathrm ds
 ={}&\int_0^1\int f\langle D,Q_s\rangle\,\mathrm ds
 +h\int_0^1\int L_{C_s}\langle D,Q_s\rangle\,\mathrm ds+o(h),\\
 \int_0^1\int\sigma^h_s|Q_s|^2\,\mathrm ds
 ={}&\int_0^1\int f|Q_s|^2\,\mathrm ds+o(1).
\end{aligned}$$ The first limit uses weak $L^2$ convergence against the fixed $L^2$ function $\langle D,Q_s\rangle$. The second uses convergence in measure and the uniform density bound, by truncation of the fixed integrable function $|Q_s|^2$. Outside the common density support both statements are vacuous.

Apply <a href="#fwd:flow-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-second">[fwd:flow-second]</a> to the two fixed endpoint functions $q_0,q_1$. Leave the endpoint contribution involving $u_B-u_A$ unexpanded. The terms of order four are then precisely <a href="#fwd:dual-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:dual-coefficient">[fwd:dual-coefficient]</a>. For the cubic term, integration by parts gives $$\int(q_1L_B-q_0L_A)
 =\int f(\langle Q_1,B\rangle-\langle Q_0,A\rangle).$$ Using $Q_1=Q_0-T_D$ and $\int_0^1Q_s\,\mathrm ds=Q_0-T_D/2$, its difference from the cubic residual is $$-\int f\left\langle B-\frac D2,T_D\right\rangle
 =-\int_0^1\int f\langle C_s,T_D\rangle\,\mathrm ds.$$ Also $q_1-q_0=-|D|^2/2$. Dropping the nonnegative last term in <a href="#fwd:young-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:young-identity">[fwd:young-identity]</a> proves <a href="#fwd:dual-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:dual-expansion">[fwd:dual-expansion]</a>.

*The radial defect.* For the radial flow use its actual velocity from <a href="#fwd:prescribed-velocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:prescribed-velocity">[fwd:prescribed-velocity]</a>, and use Lemma <a href="#fwd:flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fwd:flow">6</a> in place of Lemma <a href="#fwd:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fwd:density">8</a>. Here $D=A$, $C_s=sA$, and $Q_s=-s\nabla_AA$. The last term of <a href="#fwd:young-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:young-identity">[fwd:young-identity]</a>, divided by $h^4$, is $$\frac12\int_0^1s^2\int
     \rho^h_{s,A}|S_A+\nabla_AA|^2\,\mathrm ds
 \longrightarrow\frac16\int f|S_A+\nabla_AA|^2.$$ The integrand in the spatial limit is fixed and integrable, and <a href="#fwd:flow-density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:flow-density-bound">[fwd:flow-density-bound]</a> justifies the limit. This proves the radial assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="fwd:P-definition"></a>
We next compute the fourth-order difference. Define $$F_C:=L_CC-f\nabla_CC$$ and the separately quadratic expression $$\label{fwd:P-definition}
 P(C,C;D,D):=\int\left[
       -\langle F_C,\nabla_DD\rangle+f|\nabla_CD|^2\right].$$ The notation $P(E,F;G,H)$ means its polarization, symmetric in the first pair and separately symmetric in the second pair. Let $K_{AB}$ denote the expression in <a href="#fwd:dual-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:dual-coefficient">[fwd:dual-coefficient]</a> with $fS_E+L_EE$ replaced by $F_E$ at both endpoints.

<a id="stmt-fwd-algebra"></a>
<div id="fwd:algebra" class="lemma">

<a id="fwd:square-completion"></a>
<a id="fwd:average-P"></a>
<a id="fwd:average-curvature"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Square completion and curvature identity). *For every ordered pair, $$\label{fwd:square-completion}
 K_{AB}=\frac12\int_0^1
 \left[P(C_s,C_s;D,D)-\int f|Q_s+\nabla_{C_s}D|^2\right]\,\mathrm ds.$$ For the radial coefficients use $q_{0A}=0$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 2\mathbb E K_{0A}-\mathbb E K_{AB}
 ={}&\frac13\mathbb E
       [P(A,B;A,B)-P(A,A;B,B)]
 \nonumber\\
 &\quad+\frac12\mathbb E\int
 f|\nabla q_{AB}+\nabla_A(B-A)|^2
 \label{fwd:average-P}\\
 ={}&\frac16\mathbb ER(A,B,B,A)(f)
 +\frac12\mathbb E\int
 f|\nabla q_{AB}+\nabla_A(B-A)|^2.
 \label{fwd:average-curvature}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fwd-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-fwd-algebra</summary>

<a id="fwd:gradient-ibp"></a>
*Proof.* *Integration by parts.* We first justify the identity $$\label{fwd:gradient-ibp}
 \int\langle L_DC-L_CD,\nabla q\rangle
 =\int f\langle\nabla_DC-\nabla_CD,\nabla q\rangle,
 \qquad q\in W^{1,2}(M).$$ For test $q$, integration by parts in its two terms gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int L_D\langle C,\nabla q\rangle
 &=\int f\bigl(\langle\nabla_DC,\nabla q\rangle
                    +\operatorname{Hess}q(D,C)\bigr),\\
 \int L_C\langle D,\nabla q\rangle
 &=\int f\bigl(\langle\nabla_CD,\nabla q\rangle
                    +\operatorname{Hess}q(C,D)\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ The Hessian terms cancel. The vector fields paired with $\nabla q$ on both sides of <a href="#fwd:gradient-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:gradient-ibp">[fwd:gradient-ibp]</a> are in $L^2$ and have bounded support. Density of test functions in $W^{1,2}$ therefore proves the identity for the stated class, without assigning a Hessian to $q$.

*Square completion.* The maps $s\mapsto F_{C_s}$ and $s\mapsto Q_s$ are polynomials with values in $L^2$. They have derivatives $$F_{C_s}'=L_DC_s+L_{C_s}D-f\nabla_DC_s-f\nabla_{C_s}D,
 \qquad Q_s'=-\nabla_DD.$$ Express the endpoint difference in $K_{AB}$ as the integral of the derivative of $\langle F_{C_s},Q_s\rangle/2$, and subtract the other terms in <a href="#fwd:dual-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:dual-coefficient">[fwd:dual-coefficient]</a>. Applying <a href="#fwd:gradient-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:gradient-ibp">[fwd:gradient-ibp]</a> gives $$K_{AB}=\frac12\int_0^1\int
 \left[-\langle F_{C_s},\nabla_DD\rangle
       -2f\langle\nabla_{C_s}D,Q_s\rangle-f|Q_s|^2\right]\,\mathrm ds.$$ Completion of the square proves <a href="#fwd:square-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:square-completion">[fwd:square-completion]</a>.

*Averaging.* For the averaging, set $$T=\mathbb E P(A,A;A,A),\quad
 S=\mathbb E P(A,A;B,B),\quad
 H=\mathbb E P(A,B;A,B).$$ Independence and sign symmetry kill every term with an odd number of occurrences of either random field. Exchangeability identifies the two pure and the two unmixed terms. Expansion in the four slots therefore gives $$\mathbb E P(C_s,C_s;D,D)
 =((1-s)^2+s^2)(T+S)-4s(1-s)H.$$ Both $\int_0^1((1-s)^2+s^2)\,\mathrm ds$ and $\int_0^14s(1-s)\,\mathrm ds$ equal $2/3$. The contribution of the $P$ term to $\mathbb E K_{AB}$ is consequently $(T+S-H)/3$. In the radial case $C_s=sA$, $D=A$, $Q_s=-s\nabla_AA$, so the square vanishes and $K_{0A}=P(A,A;A,A)/6$. Finally, $$Q_s+\nabla_{C_s}D=\nabla q_{AB}+\nabla_AD.$$ These facts prove <a href="#fwd:average-P" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:average-P">[fwd:average-P]</a>.

<a id="fwd:polarized-curvature"></a>
*The curvature contraction.* To identify the curvature convention explicitly, write $$a=\nabla_AA,\qquad b=\nabla_BB,\qquad
 U=\nabla_BA,\qquad V=\nabla_AB.$$ Separate symmetric polarization of <a href="#fwd:P-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fwd:P-definition">[fwd:P-definition]</a> gives <a id="fwd:polarization"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 P(A,B;A,B)=\int\biggl[
 &-\frac14\langle L_AB+L_BA,U+V\rangle
 \nonumber\\
 &+f\left(\frac14|U|^2+\frac14|V|^2
             +\langle U,V\rangle+\frac12\langle a,b\rangle\right)
 \biggr],
 \label{fwd:polarization}
\end{aligned}$$ whereas $$P(A,A;B,B)=\int
 [-L_A\langle A,b\rangle+f\langle a,b\rangle+f|V|^2].$$ Exchangeability gives $\mathbb E|U|^2=\mathbb E|V|^2$ and $$\frac12\mathbb E\int\langle L_AB+L_BA,U+V\rangle
 =\mathbb E\int L_B\langle A,U+V\rangle.$$ Hessian symmetry for the gradient $A$ gives $\langle A,U\rangle=\langle B,a\rangle$; swapping the random choices then yields $\mathbb E\int L_B\langle A,U\rangle
 =\mathbb E\int L_A\langle A,b\rangle$. Thus $$\begin{aligned}
 2\mathbb E[P(A,B;A,B)-P(A,A;B,B)]
 =\mathbb E\int\bigl[
 &L_A\langle A,b\rangle-L_B\langle A,V\rangle
 \nonumber\\
 &+f(-\langle a,b\rangle-|V|^2+2\langle U,V\rangle)
 \bigr].
 \label{fwd:polarized-curvature}
\end{aligned}$$ The first two terms of the specified integrated curvature formula, with $(X,Y,Z,W)=(A,B,B,A)$, combine to $$L_A\langle A,b\rangle-f\langle a,b\rangle.$$ Its next two terms combine to $$-L_B\langle A,V\rangle+f\langle U,V\rangle.$$ Finally $[A,B]=V-U$, and Hessian symmetry for $B$ gives $$-f\langle\nabla_{[A,B]}B,A\rangle
